All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the Antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from around the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings Rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Josef Lenin sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bullseye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford, and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now, they ae readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. Hall was first elected secretary general of the party in 1959, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner; a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned, too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” “the decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now, the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster, or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK.” “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962, a full 17 percent of the membership was reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of Democratic Socialists of America over that same time period. In a decade, the DSA has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, DSA doesn’t just have members, it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where Democratic Socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration, but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone.’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, and coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith, and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication primarily funded by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. What Did the Kennedy Center Ever Do to Donald Trump?
2026-09-17

What Did the Kennedy Center Ever Do to Donald Trump?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Is it curtains for the Kennedy Center? Since retaking office, Donald Trump has demanded that the capital city’s chief performing arts venue be remade in his image—and threatened to shutter it if he doesn’t get his way. This week the fight came to a head: A federal judge rejected Trump’s latest efforts to rename the building after himself and demanded advance notice if he tries to knock it down. Whether Trump complies will not only determine the Kennedy Center’s fate. It’s also shaping up to be an unlikely test of whether the rule of law can constrain a rogue president. It’s the nation’s official cultural center. Created by an act of Congress and named in honor of President John F. Kennedy, the center’s white-marbled building has hosted plays, concerts, and award ceremonies since 1971. He has moved quickly to put his stamp on the center. Citing “woke” programming, he replaced its bipartisan board of trustees with sycophants last year. The new board named Trump as chairman, and he helped plan performances and awardees. An acclaimed children’s musical about a shark who “wants to let out his inner fish” was out; Sylvester Stallone was in. Dozens of prominent artists nixed performances or severed ties with the center and criticized Trump’s attempts to align it with his politics. Things turned litigious after Trump’s handpicked board voted last year to rename the center after him, adding his name to the facade and changing its online logo to “The Trump-Kennedy Center.” Not well. In May, a federal judge said that only Congress could rename the center and temporarily blocked Trump’s plan to close it for a $257 million renovation. An appeals court refused to overrule the judge. So the board came back with a new idea: appending “Restored and Renovated by President Donald J. Trump” to the center’s name and renaming its grounds for him. And the president himself issued an ultimatum: If his name doesn’t go on the building, he won’t help save it. Like many performance venues, the center’s finances suffered during the pandemic, and the administration has pointed to the threat of imminent bankruptcy to justify Trump’s takeover. But the takeover accelerated the center’s downward spiral in ticket sales and fundraising. The administration also claims that “severe structural deterioration” could require tearing down the building, citing an incident in which a chunk of ceiling collapsed during a recent storm. “I think that the Trump administration should certainly have recognition,” the president told reporters yesterday. “Because frankly, if we don’t do that, it’s going to close. It’ll end up being ripped down.” That’s a pretty naked threat. Would he really do that? If he doesn’t get his way, Trump seems more than willing to ruin the Kennedy Center for everybody else. After the same federal judge rejected the board’s latest naming workaround on Tuesday, the administration abruptly shuttered the building. (It claims the closure is temporary.) Yesterday, Trump was photographed reviewing a poster that appeared to show a plan to demolish it. On Thursday, the judge demanded 30 days’ notice before the administration makes any major structural changes to the center, including tearing it down. But Trump has taken advantage of the slow-grinding gears of our legal system before. Last year he demolished the East Wing of the White House to make way for a gargantuan ballroom before courts could stop him. Whether he takes a wrecking ball to it or lets it stagnate, the Kennedy Center threatens to become his latest victim. If that happens, it’ll not only gut a cultural landmark; it’ll be another example of the president losing in court but getting his way anyway. That’s a troubling sign for the rule of law. Last question: There’s a war on, gas is $4.43 a gallon, and the midterms are around the corner. Why is Trump so obsessed with the Kennedy Center? The fight over a performing arts venue might not matter electorally, but it’s of a piece with Trump’s personality and priorities. The president is a pop-culture fanatic; he blasts Broadway showtunes at his rallies and was tweetstorming about Kristen Stewart a few years before becoming the leader of the free world. At times, Trump has seemed more focused on his ballroom than on the war he started with Iran. It’s an object lesson that fits a broader pattern: For more than a decade now, one man’s flights of fancy have inevitably become the country’s pressing concerns. The Kennedy Center might not (yet) bear Trump’s name. But this is his world, and we’re just living in it. The potential loss of yet another national landmark got you down? For your evening enjoyment, my colleagues have some ideas about what to focus on instead. Dive into a true-crime longread: As a teenager, Winston Walters became a rising reality-TV star for wrestling giant catfish. But shortly after his 20th birthday, he was arrested for a grisly attack—which he strongly denies he committed—and sentenced to 92 years in prison. His case became a cause célèbre among fans and online content creators. Crime reporter Wes Ferguson spent months speaking with Walters, his supporters, and the crime’s victims to make sense of the case’s twists and turns. It’s a gripping read. Crack open a new book: Emily St. John Mandel, author of Station Eleven, excels at writing about alternative futures. Her latest sci-fi book, Exit Party, is no exception. According to Laura Miller, Mandel expertly weaves together an interconnected cast of characters in this “lovely, meditative new novel.” Tune in to a nuanced conversation about sex work: On the latest episode of Death, Sex & Money, host Anna Sale spoke with Anna Blake, an OnlyFans creator who had hoped the platform would be a path to financial stability. Instead, the experience forced her to change her relationship to both sex and money. End the day with some wordplay: What’s an 11-letter word for “Hogs”? And what’s a three-letter word for a “Hog’s neighbor, maybe”? Play today’s crossword for some clever clues and interesting trivia tidbits. That’s all from me. Enjoy your night and see you tomorrow! Are We Heading For “Pearl Harbor in Space”?
2026-09-17

Are We Heading For “Pearl Harbor in Space”?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink declared this week that the United States has weapons orbiting in outer space. It was the first time a U.S. official has publicly acknowledged this fact. The questions, which went unaddressed, are what sorts of weapons they are, why he’s announcing this now, and—most important—what it all means for the broader issues of war and peace. Chinese and Russian spokespeople denounced Meink’s speech as a step toward “war in outer space.” This was not only predictable but also a sick joke. Both of those countries have been launching, orbiting, and testing weapons in space—devices designed to disable or destroy U.S. satellites—for more than a decade, to a degree and with a tactical sophistication that far outpaces anything we have done. And they have been doing this without publicly declaring anything. Retired Lt. Gen. John Shaw, who was deputy commander of U.S. Space Command until 2023 and is still active on boards and companies dealing with space security, told me in a phone conversation Wednesday that Meink’s remarks marked “not an escalation but a measured response” to what China and Russia have long been doing. That can’t be said of many of America’s arms races with other nations over the past 80 years, but it seems to be the case with this one. And an arms race in space, of some sort, now appears almost inevitable. For many years, and to an extent far greater than any other country, the U.S. military has depended on assets in space. Photoreconnaissance satellites spy on what adversaries are doing. Early-warning satellites detect attacks. GPS satellites transmit signals that guide “smart bombs” to their targets, connect communications networks, even tell troops on the ground and ships at sea exactly where they are. If some of these satellites get hacked, damaged, derailed, or destroyed, America’s ability to fight a war would be seriously degraded. That seems to be the intention of Russian and Chinese “space control” programs. It is widely thought that if the U.S. ever goes to war with Russia or China, the first shots would be anti-satellite attacks, complemented by cyberattacks against a wide range of computer and communications targets. In a cyberwar, the United States has much bigger stones but much glassier houses. In other words, we have a larger, more destructive array of cyberattack tools. However, our military forces, as well as our economy and social structures, are far more dependent on software networks—and therefore much more vulnerable to cyberattacks. In outer space, according to a former defense and intelligence official who still has high-level access to information on the subject, we are not only more vulnerable but outgunned. “The U.S. is absolutely vulnerable to a Pearl Harbor in space,” this person told me. “We’re getting better, but we’re not remotely able to do what we need to do to respond to the threat.” This former official said he doesn’t understand why Meink gave this speech; he doesn’t see what we gain from it and fears that Moscow or Beijing might respond by boosting their program. However, two retired officers, each of whom has dealt with space security, disagree. First, they doubt that Moscow and Beijing can do more than they’re already doing. Second, they see Meink’s remarks as, in the words of one former officer, “strategic communications—the beginning of a discussion about deterrence.” If that is the case, even the retired officers allow that Meink could have supplied more detail and context. He made his announcement during a Monday speech in National Harbor, Maryland, at the Air, Space, and Cyber Conference of the Air and Space Forces Association—an event that would certainly be covered by specialist publications. Here were his words: We are increasing readiness against existing threats, and the United States has on-orbit space control weapons capable of defending the Joint Force against hostile adversary action. To the general reader, the sentence might seem obscure and may have been so intended. To the aficionado, however, it marked a milestone. Some time ago, U.S. officials publicly said there were systems on the ground that could jam communications to and from satellites. Now Meink was saying we have systems that can do this “on orbit”—that is, while orbiting in outer space. Nor did he mention jammers specifically. He said we had “space-control weapons” (without defining what kind) that could defend “the Joint Force” (meaning the entire array of U.S. armed forces, in space and on Earth). Space Force, the service of the armed forces that deals with this topic, has defined space control as efforts to “destroy, disrupt, or degrade” vital assets in outer space. What kinds of weapons do this? Meink didn’t say, but the range can be inferred from published accounts about the kinds of space-control weapons that Russia or China has launched and tested. These include not just jammers that block or distort a satellite’s uplinks or downlinks, but also “laser dazzlers,” which blind imaging satellites; “co-orbital interceptors,” which collide with, grapple and move, or blow up near targets; and sprays that damage satellite cameras, sensors, or solar panels. It is likely that when Meink stated that we have “on-orbit space-control weapons,” he was referring to those sorts of weapons. He was not referring to President Donald Trump’s Golden Dome missile-defense program—though some elements of it might also apply to space control. Certainly he wasn’t referring to nuclear weapons in outer space, which are banned by the 1967 Outer Space Treaty—and which would be ridiculous anyway, because the blast, radiation, X-rays, and other effects would destroy many of our own satellites, commercial and military. (For the same reason, it’s hard to take seriously reports of Russian test nukes in outer space.) According to GlobalSecurity, a private nonpartisan research organization on defense issues, it’s unlikely Meink was talking about any sorts of “kinetic” weapons—i.e., weapons that unleash bullets or bombs—since the resulting debris could also be self-destructive. (The treaty outlaws “weapons” in space but doesn’t define the term, giving us and other countries a lot of leeway.) In a roundtable after his speech, Meink declined to get more specific except to say that he wouldn’t exclude “kinetic” weapons and that the weapons, whatever they are, could be used for offensive and defensive purposes (a subtle distinction, in any case). Nor would he state how long we’ve had these things in space, how thoroughly they’ve been tested, or other details. Others noted that the main point of publicizing the program is deterrence—to make clear to adversaries that we can respond in kind to any threats they might pose, which can be done without going into specifics on exactly what we can do. Meanwhile, the former defense and intelligence official told me, “Russia and China have been doing all of this. We’ve been doing some of it for decades, but mostly as R&D. Russia and China have been launching these things and testing them in very provocative ways.” He added, “They’ve not only closed the gap—they’ve demonstrated an ability to maneuver satellites and conduct operations that we cannot do.” Not only that; military agencies aren’t able even to detect some of this activity. “Commercial satellite companies, which provide the government with services, have detected Chinese activities and have alerted us. Otherwise, we’d be clueless about a lot of what’s going on.” This evolving and now possibly accelerating arms race in space should come as no surprise. Once a realm of life becomes a “domain” for military activity, it’s all but inevitable that weapons will be deployed to protect it. Over the decades, this has happened in the air, in the oceans, and in cyberspace, so why not in space? Back in 2011, President Barack Obama signed the National Security Space Strategy, declaring that outer space—previously considered a peaceful sanctuary—was “congested, contested, and competitive.” (Actually, this too merely acknowledged what had been true for some time.) It was hoped that conflicts could be managed through diplomacy or, if that didn’t work out, through military strategy. In 2014 and 2020, U.S. and Russian diplomats met to discuss possible treaties. Shaw, then still on active duty, led the American delegation. He later wrote an op-ed for the Wall Street Journal lamenting that “the Russia delegation was merely groping for leverage against the U.S., its allies, and China.” Talks were going to continue, but Vladimir Putin’s invasion of Ukraine in 2022 put an end to that chance. China has since pulled ahead as the dominant player in this new arms race. Will Trump and Xi Jinping discuss the issue at their Washington summit next week? Doubtful, given the more urgent issues on the table—and, likely, both leaders’ reluctance to place limits on any competitive activity. As is the case with A.I., it is a shame that just as a tempting technology is advancing to new levels at once alluring and dangerous, world politics are spinning out of control, relations between the major powers are deteriorating, and the United States—at one time the country best placed to moderate at least some of these tensions—is led by a president who not only is clueless on these matters but thinks he’s a genius. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Is Aiming Again at a Familiar First-Term Target. The Consequences Could Be Deadly.
2026-09-17

Trump Is Aiming Again at a Familiar First-Term Target. The Consequences Could Be Deadly.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Last week, the Trump administration proposed a regulation that, if not undone by the president taking office in 2029, would end the uninterrupted practice—one almost as old as our nation—of collecting race and ethnicity in the decennial census. This proposal would impair public health and cost numerous Americans their lives. The most widely reported part of the regulation was the decision not to count noncitizens without green cards, a change that could have radical implications for future elections and billions in federal funding. But the race and ethnicity change is also important and could trigger large implications for public health. As we have argued in recent academic research, race and ethnicity data are key in understanding population health outcomes. Our article shows that racial health disparities come from both differences in exposure to environmental stressors and different underlying vulnerability stemming from, for instance, genetics or access to healthcare. The resulting differences can be stark. For instance, Black children are 60 percent more likely than U.S. children overall to have asthma and four and a half times more likely to die from it. Hispanic women have a 40 percent higher incidence of cervical cancer and 20 percent higher death rate from it compared to non-Hispanic white women. And Asian Americans have extremely elevated rates of certain diseases, including tuberculosis and hepatitis B. For many diseases, it is impossible or impracticable to collect detailed, local-level prevalence data. Government agencies have responded by using race and ethnicity as a proxy or input into models determining the prevalence of these health conditions. Most notably, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention combine survey data with census race and ethnicity estimates to model the prevalence of a number of chronic diseases—including asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and cancer—at a fine-grained geographic level. That granular data allows decisionmakers to allocate resources and target outreach to the areas with the highest need. For instance, Michigan has established a pilot program to place mobile health unit vans in at-risk areas. Private health systems also use this type of data frequently, with one medical system using it to concentrate services in four high-risk neighborhoods of New York City. Without collecting race and ethnicity data in the census, these estimates could not be produced, needlessly wasting resources and endangering lives. And the Trump-proposed rule makes this pernicious move despite centuries of contrary historical practice. The practice of collecting race and ethnicity data is as old as the census itself. The 1790 census differentiated between “Free White” individuals and all others, and race has appeared in every census since. The White House Office of Management and Budget promulgated the first government-wide standards in 1977, which have been used by every administration during the past five decades. The Biden administration made long-needed changes to the standards in 2024, including adding a “Middle Eastern or North African” category. That revision was undertaken with analytical rigor: The process took two years, involved 35 agencies, incorporated extensive new and existing research, and elicited over 20,000 comments. Meanwhile, the census’s proposed rule has no scientific justification or participation by federal agencies with experience in this area. And the little rationale the rule does provide does not withstand scrutiny. The Trump administration first argued that lowering the number of questions would increase response rates. Unlike the meticulous, empirically based Biden-era revision, the Trump rule cites only two studies for this proposition: One is over 30 years old, and the other is not about the census. Not to mention that the Trump proposal would simply swap out the race question for one on citizenship—making clear that the concern over response rates is a subterfuge. Second, the proposed rule suggests the collection of racial data may be unconstitutional under recent Supreme Court decisions in Students for Fair Admissions v. President and Fellows of Harvard College and Louisiana v. Callais. While both decisions cover the topic of race-based preference, neither even remotely addresses the constitutional status of data collections. Callais concerned the role of race in drawing legislative districts under the Voting Rights Act, while SFFA involved racial preferences in college admissions. Courts have made clear that collection of racial data does not raise the same constitutional concerns as racially based preferences. Even a colorblind administration should still be invested in avoiding preventable deaths. Finally, the rule argues that the collection of race and ethnicity data would continue through the Census Bureau’s yearly American Community Survey. But the two are complements, not substitutes. The ACS has an annual sample size of only 3.5 million, whereas the decennial census has universal reach. That smaller sample means certain demographic groups may be over- or under-covered, biasing the total estimates. To mitigate this problem, researchers adjust the data using the demographic data derived from the decennial census, a process that would be impossible without the race and ethnicity question. By heedlessly seeking to end a centurieslong practice, the Trump administration is headed down a dangerous path. It will compromise our ability to provide effective healthcare, lead to underinvestment in the communities that need it the most, and cost American lives. KPMG tech cuts come with a severance sum some staff call insulting
2026-09-18

KPMG tech cuts come with a severance sum some staff call insulting

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm KPMG UK is laying off staff from the Tech and Data areas of its Advisory division and offering severance terms that one affected employee described as "insulting and disgraceful." The job cuts, affecting about 4 percent of the Advisory staff, were announced internally in July. Affected workers in the AI, Cyber, SAP, and Testing teams are scheduled to leave the consulting business next month. Insiders have now shared with The Register details of the payments offered to those set to depart. The proposed redundancy package, subject to consultation and the employee signing a settlement agreement, combines statutory redundancy pay with several enhancements. The document helpfully advises staff to divide their annual salary by 52 to calculate their weekly pay. The number of weeks to which each employee is entitled depends on their age during each full year of service: half a week's pay for each year worked while under 22, one week's pay for each year worked from age 22 to 40, and 1.5 weeks' pay for each year worked from age 41. "If you turned 41 while working at KPMG, the higher rate of 1.5 weeks' pay only applies to the full year of service completed after you turned 41," KPMG's missive states. "Redundancy pay is calculated based on the individual's number of years' service (up to a maximum of 20 years' service). If you've been at KPMG for more than 20 years, your most recent 20 years of service are considered when determining the number of weeks' pay you will be entitled to." KPMG's enhancements remove the statutory weekly pay cap of £751 and count partial years of service exceeding six months. Regardless of their length of service, staff are guaranteed either £1,250 plus statutory redundancy pay or eight weeks' pay inclusive of statutory redundancy pay, whichever is higher. The agreement also provides pay in lieu of notice, including employer pension contributions and, where applicable, a car allowance, according to the document. The document explaining the package includes an illustration of three employees alongside examples of the terms they could receive. Two of the illustrated employees appear delighted. The reaction among actual employees was less cheerful. One affected worker suspected that "protecting the profit pot for the equity partners is what is driving this re-org mostly." KPMG's UK partners received an average of £880,000 in the year to September 2025. "The payout scale is insulting and disgraceful," one affected employee told The Register. A KPMG spokesperson said: "As our market evolves, we are adapting where we are focusing and how we are set up to make sure we have the right skills in place to best serve our clients. To respond to these market dynamics combined with low levels of attrition, we are proposing reductions in some of our advisory client-facing teams and will support our colleagues throughout this process." The latest cuts come six months after KPMG eliminated 600 roles across its UK organization, including around 120 in Advisory. "The firm's consulting business has been under pressure for some time as market demand has remained subdued," said Duncan Aitchison, an analyst at TechMarketView, who estimated that the practice's revenue fell 9 percent between fiscal 2024 and 2025. "KPMG is not the only Big Four firm taking action to reshape its consulting business. In April 2026, PwC UK unveiled plans to bring together two of its three advisory businesses as part of a global effort to respond to the disruptions to the consulting industry caused by AI." ® US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas Downloading a model is increasingly easy. British Army spends £16M on 1,000 pocket-sized eyes in the sky
2026-09-18

British Army spends £16M on 1,000 pocket-sized eyes in the sky

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas The British Army is buying more than 1,000 small drones for battlefield surveillance and training under contracts worth £16 million ($21 million). The Ministry of Defence (MoD) says the new portfolio of "spy" drones will give soldiers better battlefield intelligence and protection. The MoD is procuring the drones through three UK-based small and medium-sized companies, although some of the technology comes from overseas. Two models are intended for surveillance, while the third will prepare soldiers to use similar systems on operations. Most of the order consists of 670 Skydio X10 quadcopters. Each weighs about 2.49 kg (5.5 lb), can reach 45 mph, and has a flight time of up to 40 minutes. Surrey-based Marlborough Communications Limited is supplying the drones to the MoD, although their manufacturer, Skydio, is based in California. Evolve Dynamics, based in Hampshire, England, is providing 245 FOXE-NATO drones for intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance roles. The company describes the FOXE-NATO as a pouch-deployable quadcopter weighing less than 250 g (0.55 lb), designed for rapid deployment and ease of use. "This contract marks an important step in strengthening the Army's sovereign UAS (Unmanned Aerial System) capability. Our ambition is to see small, reliable uncrewed systems become standard equipment at the lowest tactical level, rather than a limited or specialist resource," Evolve Dynamics CEO Tom Redman said in a statement. The remaining 110 units are SONORA drones supplied through a partnership between Exeter-based Brigantes Consulting and Paris-headquartered Harmattan AI. Designed for training, the SONORA can be deployed in less than a minute and weighs under 1.8 kg (4 lb). The UK National Armaments Director Group handled the procurement, which forms part of Project Rapstone, an Army program intended to move new technology from industry to the front line within 24 months. The purchase follows a £400 million ($534 million) deal announced in July to replace the Army's troublesome Watchkeeper fleet with Tekever AR5 drones. The fixed-wing AR5 is much larger than the quadcopters and can carry a 50 kg (110 lb) payload while remaining airborne for up to 20 hours. "Our soldiers deserve the best technology, and that's what we're delivering," said the Minister for Defence Readiness and Industry, Luke Pollard MP. "From small drones giving troops eyes on the enemy, to the AR5 offering commanders better intelligence over the battlefield, we're transforming the Army's ability to see, understand and act first." ® US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Royal Society slams UK.gov science shake-up
2026-09-18

Royal Society slams UK.gov science shake-up

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Whitehall changes a 'big mistake' as anything going in with Business department gets squeezed The president of the Royal Society has slammed the government’s decision to move science policy from the department also covering technology to the business department. In changes announced after Andy Burnham became UK Prime Minister in July, the government abolished the Department for Science, Innovation and Technology (DSIT) – created by former Conservative Prime Minister Rishi Sunak – and split its responsibilities among other ministries. The Department for Culture, Media and Sport (DCSM) became Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport (DCMS) while the Science in DSIT went to the new Department for Business, Innovation, Science and Trade (BIST). Jonathan Reynolds was named Secretary of State for the department. Kanishka Narayan has been given a Cabinet-attending role of Minister for Artificial Intelligence, working jointly in the Cabinet Office and BIST. Chris McDonald, Minister of State for Science, Innovation and Investment is work jointly across BIST and the Department for Health and Social Care with no Cabinet role. Speaking to MPs this week, Sir Paul Nurse, the President of the prestigious Royal Society, said moving science to the business department was a mistake… “actually quite a big mistake”. Nurse, whose main research was in genetics, argued that science cuts across business, health, defense, transport, energy, and the environment. “The future of our country depends on what science can put into all that we do. Giving it just to business — there are ways through that — is restricting just what it can do, and that is a mistake. I would have put science and AI together, because AI is in fact science. I don't know why that is so neglected all the time. AI isn't just large language models and rushing after big databases. It is also about promoting creativity in science and actually generating appropriate technologies.” Nurse said experience with other government activities thrown in with business did not bode well for science. There have previously been a Department for Business, Innovation and Skills and a Department of Business, Energy and Industrial Strategy. “When you talk to the civil servants, they see the big thing about their department, which is business. So every time I had to speak to them, it was always about application to particular business objectives. They couldn't get off that. Now that is something for the Secretary of State to deal with, but that is a problem. It's not just leadership. It's also what comes underneath. And the Secretary of State has nine policy areas, of which only one is to do with science,” Nurse told the House of Commons Science and Technology Committee. Also speaking to the Committee, Alicia Greated, executive director for the Campaign for Science and Engineering, said that while there was a government connection between science and health, there was none with the Department for Education, which is responsible for universities and higher education. “That connection is extremely important. Our members are asking for stability, whether it's in funding, strategy or policy. We need to be thinking more long term. A lot of the objectives of the current departmental priorities are going to be quite short term.” Greated also said she feared a “deprioritization” of basic research. “It's not just R&D; it's the skills, it's the infrastructure, it's the ideas that feed into our whole innovation pipeline.” In a letter to the Committee, ministers from the Cabinet Office, BIST and the DCMS said: “The Prime Minister has made a number of changes to the Machinery of Government to rewire the state to deliver a stronger, more strategic center which acts as the engine room for driving economic growth across the whole country. "These changes include transforming how the Government is structured to approach science, innovation, and technology. This reflects that the UK stands at a critical juncture where unprecedented global changes demand that we secure our position as a world leader in AI, science and technology. In this context, we cannot see science and technology as an isolated issue, limited to one department.” ® US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Techie fixed Wi-Fi dead zone with a drill
2026-09-18

Techie fixed Wi-Fi dead zone with a drill

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
When wires and wireless mix, the combination can be electric Welcome to another edition of On Call, The Register's reader-contributed column that celebrates the fine art of tech support. This week, meet a reader we'll Regomize as "Eddy," the one-person IT department for an assisted living company. "I have had quite a bit of trouble creating a stable Wi-Fi network," he admitted to On Call. "The space is crowded with multiple networks because seemingly every other resident has their own router blasting Wi-Fi throughout the building," he explained. After extensive troubleshooting, he managed to establish a stable connection everywhere except one hallway at the back of the building. "I was starting to question my sanity and competence as seemingly nothing would create a stable signal," Eddy told On Call. "After beating my head on the issue for an unreasonably long time, I decided to take a break and troubleshoot another issue that a user was having: Their monitors would cut out randomly, more so if someone quickly closed the office door." Eddy examined the desk, where a stand held a pair of monitors, whose power and video cables all snaked downwards through the same hole in the desk's surface. "When the stand moved, it was also crimping the power cords," Eddy observed. "Jiggle the desk or slam the door and the monitors would cut out briefly." He drilled a new hole for the stand to sit in and replaced the power cables. That fixed the monitors – and, unexpectedly, restored the Wi-Fi around the desk. "As near as I can tell, the power cables were crimped just enough that they started to transmit and interfere with the Wi-Fi," Eddy told On Call. However, Eddy's other Wi-Fi dead zone remained. He signed off his email to The Register: "Now I am going to be haunting the back hallway with a software-defined radio dongle and waving around a Yagi antenna like a demented wizard to see if I can find the second source of interference." Have you fixed one problem and found it also fixed another? AI risks make some insurers wary of corporate liability
2026-09-18

AI risks make some insurers wary of corporate liability

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
RAND wants better data to price machine-made mishaps, apocalypse not included If AI does end up killing us all, or just compounds our daily havoc, the insurance industry would rather not pay the bill. "AI-related harms are already emerging, including incorrect or misleading outputs, deepfakes, privacy violations, intellectual property disputes, fraud, product defects, and discriminatory decisions," observes the RAND Corporation, a non-profit research think tank, in a newly issued report. "These harms create demand for insurance but do not fit neatly within existing insurance lines." Companies want to protect themselves from the financial liability of deploying unreliable, mendacious, amoral AI agents, but insurance companies have become wary about taking on that risk. And if AI ends up being uninsurable, the AI industry will have to moderate its ambitions and sales targets while corporate customers delay AI projects to fulfill their fiduciary obligations. RAND says its report was motivated by the mismatch between rapid enterprise adoption of AI and the fragmented market for insuring or not insuring AI usage, particularly in the US. Apocalyptic scenarios aside, the report says that companies are already grappling with AI-related incidents and related litigation while insurers, regulators, brokers, and policyholders puzzle over risks, coverage, and rules. Insurer W. R. Berkley has already introduced exclusions in its D&O (directors and officers), E&O (errors and omissions), and Fiduciary Liability insurance products to exclude coverage for "any actual or alleged use, deployment, or development of Artificial Intelligence." During the company's Q4 2025 earnings call, CEO W. Robert Berkley highlighted the need for underwriters to understand "the impact that [new technologies like AI are] having on our insureds, what it means for risk, and our ability to fully understand that risk so we can control it, select it, and price for it." The RAND report observes that some insurance carriers are excluding AI-related harms, noting that in January 2026, Verisk/ISO – whose standardized forms appear in more than 80 percent of US property and casualty policies – introduced optional language carriers can adopt to exclude bodily injury, property damage, and other harms arising from generative AI. Not all insurers are deciding not to cover AI harms. As RAND points out, coverage gaps are being filled by new and existing companies that believe they have a handle on the risk calculations. To illustrate those risks, the report points to the Artificial Intelligence Incident Database (AIIDB) which lists tral-world harm or near-harm caused by generative AI. At the time of writing, the AIIDB lists 713 incidents drawn from more than 6,000 reports and covers AI use beyond chatbots. Among these generative AI events, the categorical breakdown includes: misinformation and manipulation (586), deepfakes and synthetic media (346), deepfake enabled misinformation (333), hallucination and factual error (215), harmful content (92), agentic and autonomous failure (84), privacy and data leak (58), bias and discrimination (47), copyright and IP (20), other and unclassified (14), and wrongful AI attribution (7). The total of those events exceeds 713 because some incidents span multiple categories. Then there are the 250 or so US lawsuits related to AI, largely related to copyright and IP, but also touching on privacy/surveillance, fraud and deception, negligence and product liability, discrimination and civil rights, and contracts/trade secrets. And after that, the report touches on the dozens of laws in various US states covering AI intimate images, AI-generated child sexual abuse material (CSAM), automated decision making, AI in political ads, and related rules that could trip up businesses deploying AI. In light of all these potential problems, one might assume this AI thing is dead in the water. But no. Businesses keep deploying AI, despite potential uncertainty about whether corporate insurance policies protect them. To address the gaps in the market, RAND argues that policy researchers and brokers, carriers, and reinsurers need to develop a common taxonomy to track AI incidents and claims. And the think tank wants to see state regulators push for an AI Coverage Notice so everyone is clear on what's covered and what isn't. RAND expects AI insurance coverage will move beyond being a specialty product. The report argues that the risks just need to be understood and priced accordingly. But that will take time. The AI industry may get the pause it has been asking for as insurers puzzle over how to price the end of humanity. ® US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Understanding how it was made, or changing a system at its root, is another matter Whitehall changes a 'big mistake' as anything going in with Business department gets squeezed It might be the next trillion-dollar company, but it'll need some help on the supply side to do it 960DT is set to arrive early, boasting performance far exceeding anything the West could offer the Middle Kingdom Lasso Security sees differences in tool handling and model refusals GPUzilla woos neoclouds into another walled garden, promising smarter, more efficient, and profitable bit barns Data protection chiefs call for 'immediate review' of data protection models PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' AWS confesses its console causes cloudy confusion for new users
2026-09-18

AWS confesses its console causes cloudy confusion for new users

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Improved signup experience for ‘AI builders’ hides complexity and includes spending cap Amazon Web Services does a lot of things right, but the cloud giant’s user console is arguably not one of them. Which may be why it’s just created an easier alternative for new users. In a Wednesday post, Senior Solutions Architect Micah Walter said that when AWS launched its earliest storage, compute, and queuing services, “anyone with an idea could start building.” Over the years, AWS added more services, and more options, making its console and overall UI quite complex. “That combination of global reach, breadth, and depth remains essential for those customers,” Walter wrote, “but if you are at the start of a new idea, every configuration option is effort standing in the way of shipping your dream product fast.” “We’ve heard from builders that they do not want to spend their first hours configuring an AWS environment,” he added. The cloud giant’s response is a new “getting started experience” aimed at “builders who are working at the pace of AI.” The Register understands that AWS has been working on this for almost a year, after realizing new users find its existing console intimidating. Walter said the new UI allows users to establish an AWS account with credentials from Google, GitHub, and Apple. When new users sign up, AWS will automatically create a project. “Instead of having to complete configuration tasks before you can work on your project, you start with sensible defaults and simple administration,” Walter wrote. Once logged in, users “get a prompt to paste into your coding agent that conﬁgures it to work with your new AWS environment. From there, your agent can deploy resources, run workloads, and iterate on your application following best practices for working with AWS.” The cloud colossus will apply what Walter described as “sensible defaults” to resources, and those who sign up with the tool can also apply a spending limit starting from $20 a month. AWS will “suggest a spend limit based on your usage, and you can accept that recommendation or set a custom amount if you are planning to further scale your usage,” Walter wrote. Users will also get warnings if they approach their spending ceiling, and AWS will pause a project rather than let it exceed a user’ budget. AWS has always tried to win new users and startups with free services and onboarding assistance. The Register cannot recall a moment when the cloud giant created an entirely new user experience to woo them, or all-but admitted that its current console is an impediment to adoption. ® US spy-tech firm insists its new UK senior vice president is there to 'build things, not to open doors' AI, Cyber, SAP and Testing teams caught in latest reshaping of Big Four consultancy's Advisory arm PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Surveillance and training drone systems will come through three UK suppliers, though some are built overseas Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Flash floods can strike without warning — this new technology could change that
2026-09-18

Flash floods can strike without warning — this new technology could change that

The Verge | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
﻿Satellite data combined with machine learning gives meteorologists new tools to predict deadly floods sooner. ﻿Satellite data combined with machine learning gives meteorologists new tools to predict deadly floods sooner. On the morning of June 9th, Laura Lin was working from her home in Lanesville, a rural southern Indiana town about 15 miles from the Kentucky border. She was on a Zoom call, unaware that the heavy rain outside was beginning to flood her yard. “I look over to where the barn is over there, and I see pieces of my wood floating, and I was like, ‘What?’ And I immediately was like, ‘I have to go.’ Close my laptop, and I get my kids up, and I’m like, ‘Something’s wrong,’” Lin recalls. Lin and her family got out safely and sheltered at a neighbor’s house, but Lanesville got over 8 inches of rain within just a few hours that day, way over the threshold for what is considered “heavy” rainfall. The water was gone a few hours later. Floods are the second-deadliest type of weather event in the United States and the deadliest globally. Just 6 inches of fast-flowing water can knock an adult off their feet, 12 inches can lift a car, and 2 feet can move larger vehicles like trucks and SUVs. A warming climate means more instances of extreme rainfall in the US, creating more opportunities for floods to occur. The deadly flash flooding in Nepal in August caused by a glacier collapse shows the power of water to devastate communities worldwide. Fortunately, new tools are being developed to counter this threat. A new software called the Transient Artifact and Continuous Learning System (TACLS) uses satellites and machine learning to try to spot areas that could flood sooner and help the National Weather Service (NWS) make better decisions when it comes to issuing flash flood alerts. “We didn’t get the ‘get on your roof’ warnings until I was already at that person’s house,” Lin says. “All the people in town were already flooded when they started sending out alerts, being like, ‘Get on your roof. We’re dispatching boats.’ So it was way too late.” TACLS has the potential to help change that. “It will help you save lives,” says Ivory Small, science and operations officer at the NWS San Diego Weather Forecast Office. Without TACLS, Small says, a storm moving into your area “could kill some folks.” With TACLS, “you can put out the warning and save some folks.” Weather forecast offices are responsible for issuing severe weather alerts, including flash flood warnings. There are 122 of them throughout the US and its territories, says Jayme Laber, senior service hydrologist for the NWS Weather Forecast Office in Oxnard, California, which oversees the LA area. Small and Laber worked closely on the TACLS project with a broader team of scientists and researchers, and they hope this new project will streamline weather alerts. To understand TACLS, it’s important to first understand how the NWS issues flood alerts. Currently, the people working in weather forecast offices use a variety of tools to monitor flood conditions. They rely on rain and stream gauges to tell them how much water is in a certain place at any given time, Laber says. They track developing storms using satellites and weather radars. The staff monitors rainfall, but their computers also alert them when it rains more than what is considered safe for a specific area. “Based on the type of soils we have, the steepness of slopes, we have developed flash flood guidance so that we know that if we get this amount of rain in this amount of time in that area, it could result in flash flooding,” Laber says. All of that, plus the years of expertise inside those buildings, determines whether or not someone presses a button to issue a flood alert for their area, Laber says. A watch is the least serious alert. It means that there’s a chance a flash flood could happen. The watch might be issued anywhere from 12 to 48 hours in advance, Laber explains. He says local emergency teams should start working on “flood protection measures” when the flood watch is issued, so there’s enough time to prepare before people are stuck and forced to find higher ground. Response teams might review evacuation plans, set up sandbags and road barriers, and identify the highest risk areas ahead of a potential flood. An advisory is the next level up. It might be flooding, “but it’s more nuisance-level. It’s not going to threaten life or property,” Laber says. A warning is the most serious alert. “That’s a life-and-death possibility of being swept off your feet [or] being run over by a car floating down the stream,” Small says. The NWS differentiates a regular flood from a flash flood based on time, says Small. If an area floods in under six hours, it’s a flash flood. The NWS has a lot of different ways to determine whether or not it needs to issue a flash flood warning, but its tech has limitations, says Yehuda Bock, TACLS project lead and research geodesist at the Scripps Institution of Oceanography at the University of California, San Diego. The satellites the NWS uses are more detailed over oceans than land, he says. And tracking rainfall as it’s happening doesn’t give you enough lead time, “because once there’s precipitation, you’re already in the event itself,” he explains. Deserts and other less populated areas typically have fewer rain gauges, Laber says. “We have a fairly dense network [of rain gauges], but it doesn’t cover every inch of the Earth.” TACLS uses satellites and machine learning to identify places on a map — for now, just in California — that risk transitioning from rain to dangerous flash floods. But soon, the technology will be available to every NWS office. Scientists from UCSD, the NWS, and NASA worked together to create TACLS. NASA’s Earth Science Technology Office funded the project through its Advanced Information Systems Technology program. TACLS relies on the Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS), which is primarily used to predict earthquakes, says Bock. But the GNSS network, which consists of satellites and ground sensors, also happens to provide data that can help forecasters decide if they need to issue a flash flood warning. The more water vapor, or moisture, there is in the atmosphere, the longer the delay in communication between the GNSS satellites and the sensors on the ground. The amount of moisture, or “precipitable water,” in the atmosphere tells the NWS what is actually happening in the skies leading up to a storm, Laber says. TACLS uses satellites and machine learning to identify places that risk transitioning from rain to dangerous flash floods. Before TACLS, Laber and his colleagues had to rely on the forecast, which wasn’t always accurate. TACLS provides real-time data about the precipitable water in the atmosphere to compare to the forecast. This helps them determine whether the storm is actually moving at the speed predicted by the weather models. “It helps us to make better decisions in our warning decision process,” Laber says. Even with the GNSS moisture data, forecasters still need something to analyze all of it. Bhavik Chandna, a UCSD graduate student, spent about a year developing the machine learning side of TACLS using what’s called long short-term memory architecture. According to Chandna, this specific machine learning system works especially well for things like developing storms and other weather patterns that change over time. Years of atmospheric measurements from the GNSS network, as well as data about atmospheric rivers, precipitation, and flash flood warnings, were used to train the machine learning model to understand moisture changes in the atmosphere “as a storm develops,” Chandna explains. At the same time, the machine learning system was trained to determine if the conditions are right to issue a flash flood warning. “Machine learning sucks at some things and excels at some things,” says Joel Johnson, associate professor in the department of earth and planetary sciences at the University of Texas at Austin. Johnson, who was not involved in the TACLS project, knows firsthand the challenges of working with these types of tools. “In general, it’s very easy to have artifacts in your data, false positives. A sensor goes funky, and things like that,” Johnson says. Still, he says TACLS seems “well suited” to the task of predicting flash floods. False positives do happen, Chandna says. “A real weather system usually affects an area, not just one isolated GNSS station. If one station shows a strong signal but its neighbors do not, that detection can be suppressed. If several nearby stations see the same pattern, we have much more confidence that it represents a real atmospheric signal,” he adds. Chandna says TACLS is not intended to replace the forecasters who issue weather alerts. It’s just another piece of information that can assist in their decision-making, he says. TACLS software is already being used in the LA and San Diego weather forecast offices where flash flooding is a common occurrence, says Laber. “In the West, we deal with a lot more flash flooding, where it happens a lot quicker,” he explains. Today, TACLS shows them a graph with various points telling them what is going on in real time. A newer version of TACLS with improved graphics is currently being loaded onto the NWS system and is nearly done, Laber says. It will be available to all of the NWS offices in the second half of October, he adds. This iteration has a map with detailed graphics showing how much it’s raining and whether an extreme weather event is occurring. According to Laber, having more tools — like this latest update to TACLS — will further improve their decision-making when it comes to issuing flash flood alerts, and, in turn, maybe speed up alert times so people know there’s a problem sooner. Laber says TACLS will likely be used the most in the Western US because the majority of the GNSS sensors are located in places known for earthquake activity. But Chandna thinks TACLS could work well elsewhere too. As long as there are some GNSS sensors and available local weather data, “we could use the same ideology that we have in our system around different parts of the world,” he says. Small is thinking beyond TACLS. He believes ongoing research is crucial to improving how the NWS issues flash flood warnings. “We learn what’s going on a little more every time we do a storm because they’re all different,” he says. “It’s imperative that you do the research after, especially big events, and see what caused it to do what it did, and why, if it was different than what we already knew, did this occur.” Lin’s concern is Lanesville and her home. “It would have been helpful to know [water was coming] before I was locked in. I couldn’t go anywhere,” Lin says. “If I got an alert that was like, ‘You need to get out now if you’re in lower-lying ground, this is coming.’ Yeah, that would have [been helpful]. But it came in so fast.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Waymo says Singapore will be its next international robotaxi city

The Verge | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The Alphabet-owned company says it will launch a commercial robotaxi service in Singapore in 2028. The Alphabet-owned company says it will launch a commercial robotaxi service in Singapore in 2028. Waymo says it will launch a robotaxi service in Singapore in 2028, as the Alphabet-owned company continues to eye overseas markets for expansion. Waymo’s vehicles will begin arriving in Singapore in “the coming months,” the company says, in preparation of mapping and autonomous testing with human safety drivers behind the wheel in 2027. Waymo says it is “working toward offering fully autonomous ride-hailing to the public” by 2028. Waymo will need approval from Singapore’s Land Transport Authority (LTA) before it can begin accepting passengers. The city-state requires all autonomous vehicles to undergo a safety assessment conducted at the Centre of Excellence for Testing and Research (CETRAN) before they can be deployed on public roads. The tests have been developed by LTA and CETRAN, with input from the Traffic Police. But Waymo isn’t totally unknown in Singapore. The company established a corporate entity there earlier this year and participates in Singapore’s Steering Committee on Autonomous Vehicles. Singapore has become a hotbed for autonomous vehicle testing. The local authorities have greenlit autonomous freight delivery and road-sweepers, as well as self-driving buses for airport workers. Motional, a US-based company majority owned by Hyundai, conducted a robotaxi pilot in Singapore in 2016. In addition to Singapore, Waymo has said it plans on launching a robotaxi service in London by the end of 2026, and Tokyo in 2027. The company has also said it will bring its robotaxis to Munich, Germany, in late 2027, marking its first expansion in the European Union. “Singapore has built one of the safest, most efficient, and most forward-thinking transportation ecosystems in the world,” Waymo co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana said in a statement. “Their focus on reliability, sustainability, and human-centric progress directly mirrors how we have built Waymo’s technology and service.” This year, Waymo has announced several new domestic markets, including Nashville, Denver, San Diego, and Las Vegas. The company has around 4,000 vehicles operating in about 15 US cities. But regulatory hurdles and political opposition have kept it out of some of the US’ most important cities, including New York City, Chicago, and Washington, DC. As the robotaxi debate continues to rage on in America, Waymo has signaled that it intends to keep growing overseas — with no signs of slowing down. A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
