Executives from BYD, CATL and Xiaomi may join Xi’s Washington visit, Reuters reports
2026-09-18

Executives from BYD, CATL and Xiaomi may join Xi’s Washington visit, Reuters reports

The Next Web | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
US President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping at the Temple of Heaven in Beijing, on May 14, 2026, during Trump’s state visit to China. Xi Jinping is scheduled to be in Washington on September 24 for a state visit, and some of China’s largest manufacturers may accompany him, Reuters reported. The information on which ones is still unknown, and the trip itself has not been officially confirmed. According to Reuters, citing three individuals familiar with the situation, the seven companies under consideration for the delegation could be the carmaker BYD, the battery manufacturers CATL and Gotion, the phone and electric-vehicle firm Xiaomi, the consumer-electronics company Hisense, the auto-parts company Wanxiang, and Bank of China. The companies that are to go are expected to attend a White House state dinner, the two sources stated. Still, the list has not yet been finalized, and invitations are due to be sent out within a few days, with no individual executives named in the report. BYD refused to comment to Reuters; the other six did not reply to the agency’s requests for comment, and we have not independently verified the report. The South China Morning Post listed the companies first, having reported a day earlier that executives from BYD, Xiaomi, the optical components company Zhongji Innolight, and the Bank of China were expected to travel, based on information from two individuals who were aware of the situation. The agenda includes an agreement involving the Board of Trade, licenses for the export of rare earths to American companies, and an extension of the trade truce obtained in October. It is up to the meeting between the US Treasury Secretary, Scott Bessent, and Vice Premier He Lifeng over the weekend to determine whether any of these points will be realized. The White House has rejected a proposed roundtable meeting between the chief executives of the US and China, and no meetings organized by think-tanks or business groups are scheduled. Chinese companies have been instructed to get ready until it is confirmed. A guest list of that kind would be rather unusual for a state dinner. Both CATL and BYD are on the Pentagon’s list of Chinese military companies, a classification that BYD has threatened to challenge legally and that CATL is currently appealing. Gotion is involved in a legal dispute with Michigan officials over a $2.36bn battery plant that was blocked. Wanxiang paid $53m to settle customs violations. Chinese electric vehicles already face 100% US tariffs, in addition to restrictions on their software and hardware, meaning that several of the companies reportedly invited to the dinner are precisely the ones the tariffs were designed to target. It has been the Americans who have been more outspoken. Trump set the date himself in July and has now confirmed to Reuters that Xi will receive a state dinner. Beijing has said nothing; the Chinese foreign ministry stated that it has no information on the delegation, and the diplomatic timetable it published does not include the visit. Both the State Department and the White House refused to give further comments to Reuters. The two accounts state that the invitations were sent within a few days, and until they are issued, the delegation is just a list of companies that have been informed to be ready. The Next Web
Google Home opens up to Claude, OpenClaw and any other MCP agent
2026-09-18

Google Home opens up to Claude, OpenClaw and any other MCP agent

The Next Web | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
“Connecting a real-life home to an AI agent allows that agent to control devices on your behalf,” Google warns in its developer documentation. “Depending on your agent, connecting it to Home MCP can result in unexpected or even undesired behaviour.” That warning sits inside the setup guide for Google Home MCP. Google opened it to early access on 16 September. It lets outside AI agents read and control a Google Home. Any agent that supports the Model Context Protocol can connect. Google names Antigravity, Claude, Hermes and OpenClaw. A connected agent reaches every device and the event history behind it. That runs from Nest doorbells and thermostats to Matter light bulbs. Google listed four things its early testers built. An agent can summarise footage across cameras. A parent can ask what the children did when they got home, and get the clips back. It can also read device state history. That covers how many loads of laundry ran in a week, or how long the lights stayed on. Then it can speak to you. An agent that finishes a long task can push an audio message through a Google Home speaker. It can also generate a custom dashboard, replacing the Google Home app’s own interface with one an agent built. “This gives your favourite AI agent real-world physical context,” Taylor Lehman, group product manager for Google Home and Nest, wrote in the announcement. The reach is consumer. The installation is not. A user creates a Google Cloud project, enables the Home API, then configures an OAuth consent screen. After that comes a client ID and secret, the right redirect URI for their agent, and publishing the app. Google suggests asking the agent itself to do the configuration. The prompt it supplies for that still points at a preprod sandbox endpoint, which tells you how early this is. Early access covers Google Home Premium Advanced subscribers in the United States, in English. That is the $20 a month tier, CNET reported. TechCrunch asked about other tiers and markets, and Google would not say. Automations are missing too. Creating and managing them through Home MCP is not supported yet, Google says. It also flags latency and experimental device traits as known issues. Some limits are hard. Home MCP enforces rate limits and blocks sensitive actions. Google gives unlocking doors as its example of what an agent may not do, and access can be revoked from the Google Home app at any time. Facial recognition needs a second, separate consent. A user must be a manager of the home structure. They must also run a compatible Nest camera or doorbell with familiar face detection on, then visit a consent link tied to their own client ID and home. Google also asks people to tell the rest of the household. If a home is shared, it says, the others should know that your agent can control devices and read home data. Its suggested alternative is to create a separate home for testing. Handing an agent the keys to a house arrives with a known problem. Prompt injection hides instructions inside ordinary content, which the agent then follows. CNET notes that researchers demonstrated it against early versions of Gemini in the smart home, and that Google patched those. The pattern is not theoretical elsewhere. Spain’s data protection authority reported its first breach by an agent this week. In August, researchers at Zenity found malicious AI skills with 1.7 million installs. Attackers have already tricked a chatbot into hijacking accounts. None of that makes Home MCP unsafe. It moves the safety question from Google to whichever agent a user connects, and Google says as much in its own warning. Apple Home and Amazon Alexa have shipped nothing like this. That makes Google the first of the three large platforms to open a house to third-party agents, CNET reported. Enthusiasts have done it for years through Home Assistant, but that route requires the sort of person who already runs Home Assistant. Europe gets none of it yet. The flagship demonstration here is an agent summarising camera footage of children. The questions about what leaves the home, and on whose terms, are sharper on this side of the Atlantic, and Google has not said when it will answer them. SoftBank expands $6.05bn credit facility to $6.5bn with 20-plus banks
2026-09-18

SoftBank expands $6.05bn credit facility to $6.5bn with 20-plus banks

The Next Web | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Masayoshi Son, founder and current chief executive officer of Japanese holding conglomerate SoftBank. SoftBank Group has added $450m to an existing credit line, lifting the facility to $6.5bn, people familiar with the matter told Bloomberg. Set against what the company is spending on OpenAI, the sum is a rounding error. Set against the week SoftBank has just had, it is more like a seatbelt. The previous facility, worth $6.05bn, was due to expire this month, and the expanded line gives SoftBank another year to draw on it, Bloomberg reported on September 18. More than 20 banks are involved, and the new money carries an interest margin of 210 basis points over the Secured Overnight Financing Rate. Whether SoftBank has drawn anything or intends to is unknown. A company representative declined to comment to Bloomberg. TNW has not independently verified the reporting, which rests on people who asked not to be identified because the arrangement is private. Commitment lines of this kind are revolving facilities that lenders keep available for a set period. Borrowers treat them as liquidity insurance rather than funding, and frequently never touch them. Everything else on SoftBank’s balance sheet is the opposite. The company committed in February to a further $30bn in OpenAI, in three installments of $10bn, the last of them due on October 1. That takes its cumulative investment to $64.6bn, with an interest rate of roughly 13%, at a $730bn pre-money valuation, SoftBank said at the time. The money, it added, would come initially from bridge loans. It duly did. SoftBank signed a $40bn bridge loan in March, drew $30bn of it, and repaid the $25.9bn outstanding this week, per Bloomberg. Since then, it has taken an $11.87bn term loan and a $10bn loan secured on its OpenAI stake. On the same day the credit line was reported, Bloomberg also reported that SoftBank had raised its Arm-backed margin loan by $5bn to $25bn. Apollo Global Management, separately, is in talks to increase its own loan to $9bn from $5.4bn. There may be more. SoftBank has spent the week meeting with fixed-income investors in New York to gauge appetite for a $10bn to $20bn junk bond, according to Bloomberg, which reported that a deal could reach the market as early as next week. The company described those meetings as non-deal and said nothing had been determined on any bond issuance. The remaining $10bn of the bridge loan can next be drawn in early October. The backdrop to all of it is a market that has grown noticeably less relaxed about debt-funded AI, with OpenAI’s own listing pushed back on safety grounds. The cost of insuring SoftBank’s debt against default hit a three-year high earlier this week. A standby facility that nobody may ever draw on is not the largest number Masayoshi Son signed off this month. On current evidence, it may be the most carefully timed. Apple Pay is heading to India next month with Axis Bank first, Reuters reports
2026-09-18

Apple Pay is heading to India next month with Axis Bank first, Reuters reports

The Next Web | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Apple Pay is expected to launch in India next month, beginning with credit cards issued by Axis Bank, three people familiar with the plans told Reuters. Apple declined to comment. So, in effect, did everybody else. The service will widen to other lenders through separate bank-by-bank agreements, the pattern Apple has followed in other markets, Reuters reported on September 18. Apple has also held talks with HDFC Bank and ICICI Bank, both larger card issuers than Axis, but has not settled commercial terms with either. Apple and HDFC Bank declined to comment to Reuters, and Axis Bank and ICICI Bank each said they had no comment. Axis is India’s fourth-largest credit card issuer, with 16.26 million active cards as of July, according to the same report. It is a meaningful book without being one of the two that set the market price, which is a useful place to start if the price is the thing still being argued about. And it has been argued about for a while. Bloomberg first reported in February that Apple was in talks with Indian banks about launching a payments service. The Economic Times reported in August that the target was October, that the launch would cover Visa and Mastercard credit cards only, and that Apple had sought 15 to 20 basis points of each transaction while banks countered at around 10. The October timing has since been matched by several Indian outlets, along with the detail that lands hardest: no support for UPI at launch. India’s state-backed instant payments rail accounts for about 84% of digital payment volume, according to Reuters. Apple is entering a country with a card product that has largely stopped reaching for cards. What changed is the plumbing underneath. The Reserve Bank of India’s authentication directions, issued in September 2025 and in force since April, allow card issuers to offer customers a choice of authentication factors, including device-native biometrics, rather than mandating an SMS one-time password for every transaction. That is the rule that allows Face ID or Touch ID to authorize a payment on an Indian bank card, and its absence is a major reason Apple Pay could not come to India earlier. The commercial case has improved alongside the regulatory one. Apple’s share of the Indian smartphone market has doubled to 8% since 2022, Reuters reported, a small slice of a very large market that skews toward the exact customers banks issue premium credit cards to. Apple has run this play before, one market at a time, on terms it negotiates privately with each issuer. What it has not done is announce a date. Neither has Axis Bank. For now, the most concrete metric for Apple Pay in India is the card count. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.
